# Frame baseline log

Every accepted change, with the reason. A baseline moved without a reason here is a regression nobody noticed.

- **2026-08-02 16:02** · `shipwright` — Weathered sailcloth reaches the artefact at last: flax 0.815 (near paper) to 0.680 with grime accumulating from the foot and leeches inward, applied to web/shaders/SAIL_FRAG.frag.glsl, which is the source of truth. Also the first frame in which the yards are actually braced. Diff is confined to the sails: hulls, deck, sea and panels are unchanged.
- **2026-08-02 16:02** · `action` — Same sailcloth and yard-bracing change seen at sea. 0.079 percent, confined to the canvas of the engaged ships.
- **2026-08-02 16:35** · `ship-dhow` — New frame. The Shipwright is now addressable by hull via #v=ship&s=<id>, so a baseline can target a specific ship instead of whatever the view opens on. This one covers the settee rig, which the audit found the code had been building as a lateen triangle.
- **2026-08-02 16:35** · `ship-junk` — New frame. Covers the battened lug, where the battens must stay inside the canvas when the sail is sheeted out — the fault August reported on 2026-08-02.
- **2026-08-02 16:39** · `ship-dhow` — Re-baselined against the pinned Shipwright camera. The first baseline was captured mid-pan because the view's eased pan and zoom was not frozen; it is now, so the framing is stable. Also carries settee 0.30.
- **2026-08-02 16:39** · `ship-junk` — Re-baselined against the pinned Shipwright camera; the first was captured mid-pan.
- **2026-08-02 16:48** · `ship-junk` — Card prose only. The junk card no longer claims the battened lug among China's firsts: junks carried square sails until about the 12th century and then adopted the tanja and the fully battened rig from the southern seas. Diff confined to the WHAT SHE WAS paragraph; no geometry moved.
- **2026-08-03 00:02** · `ship-dhow` — New triangular-sail geometry. The old belly was a product of two parabolas, zero on every edge, which pinned the free leech flat. Now draft sits 38-40 percent aft of the luff, the leech sags and twists more aloft than low down, and there are corner creases, luff lacing scallops and a loose foot.

- **2026-08-03 15:10** · `ship-great-eastern` — Paddle box added over the top half of each wheel, with vent slats and a tagged sponson; floats darkened from new timber to weathered board. Diff confined to the wheel area.
- **2026-08-03 15:46** · `ship-great-eastern` — Superstructure relief: window mullions at a fixed real spacing, stanchions and three rails along each deckhouse edge, and cowl ventilators. globe-default's 1.087 percent on the cold run was the known webfont race and settled to 0.000 on re-run.
- **2026-08-03 16:19** · `ship-great-eastern` — Paddle wheel and box raised: the axle was a metre BELOW the waterline, submerging a 17 m wheel by more than a radius. Now derived from stated quantities (waterline = sheer less freeboard) and measured at 6.36 m above water, dipping 12.6 percent of the diameter. Company band also tagged.
- **2026-08-03 16:51** · `ship-great-eastern` — Paddle wheel re-oriented into the fore-and-aft plane (measured 17.7 m x 17.6 m x 7.9 m), sponson moved from the wheel centre to deck level, funnel livery rebuilt as vertex-coloured paint on one cylinder (buff with black top) removing the proud band ring, salmon anti-fouling and a gold sheer line. All against the museum model August supplied.
- **2026-08-03 17:08** · `ship-great-eastern` — Deckhouse lofted to the hull's own plan form instead of a constant-width box, so it can no longer overhang the side or run past the stem. Measured: house half-width 11.77 m inside a 12.65 m half-beam; forward end 946.7 against a stem at 926.0. Paddle wheels now turn at a rate derived from ship speed and wheel radius.

- **2026-08-03 22:12** · `ship-great-eastern` — Deckhouse windows painted into the house's own lofted geometry as vertex colours, removing the constant-width window strip and mullion boxes that were coincident with a tapering house. August confirmed these were flickering.
- **2026-08-03 22:12** · `ship-titanic` — Two pole masts with a crow's nest — she had masts=[] and therefore no masts at all — plus the same deckhouse window fix.
- **2026-08-03 23:25** · `ship-great-eastern` — Windows restored. The first vertex-colour attempt painted a band at 0.30-0.66 of tier height onto a wall whose only vertices were at sole and roof, so no vertex was ever in the band and the windows vanished. - **2026-08-04 02:09** · `ship-canoe` — Two deliberate changes, both structural. (1) The fleet is 25 vessels, not 24: the dugout canoe had no hull spec, which is why era 0 had no shipping at all, and inserting it at the head shifts every entry in the fleet list, the bottom strip and the to-scale fleet line by one slot. (2) Wave amplitude now follows Hs ~ 0.021 U^2 instead of A*(0.30+0.070U), which varied wave height only 1.7x across the whole range this model sees; at the default 7 m/s the table's stated amplitudes are now used as written rather than silently reduced to 79 per cent. The scaling was written in three places and is now folded into the uniform before upload, so the shaders have no wind term left to disagree about.

- **2026-08-04 08:46** · `shipwright` — Carries the same two changes already accepted for the other seven ship frames last round, which this frame was not listed in: the fleet is 25 vessels not 24 (the dugout had no hull, which is why era 0 had no shipping), shifting every list entry and the whole to-scale fleet line by one slot; and wave amplitude now follows Hs ~ 0.021 U^2 rather than a law that varied wave height only 1.7x across the entire range this model sees. - **2026-08-04 08:46** · `globe-default` — The three ocean layers rebuilt. What lives in the water was a MEASURED dead switch - toggling it moved 0.16 per cent of pixels by a mean of 0.008 of a level - because its ramp put open ocean at 0.12 against an OFF value of 0.16, so across the gyres it was a no-op by construction; rebuilt, 11.6 per cent of water pixels now move over the Benguela and the upwelling ribbon is legible. Wind streaks elongated from 6.8:1 to 19:1 with a third less amplitude, because the eye reads wind from elongation rather than contrast. Era fleets additionally sit on the sphere rather than in orbit, and ships beyond the true horizon acos(R/d) are culled.

- **2026-08-04 08:46** · `globe-modern` — The three ocean layers rebuilt. What lives in the water was a MEASURED dead switch - toggling it moved 0.16 per cent of pixels by a mean of 0.008 of a level - because its ramp put open ocean at 0.12 against an OFF value of 0.16, so across the gyres it was a no-op by construction; rebuilt, 11.6 per cent of water pixels now move over the Benguela and the upwelling ribbon is legible. Wind streaks elongated from 6.8:1 to 19:1 with a third less amplitude, because the eye reads wind from elongation rather than contrast. Era fleets additionally sit on the sphere rather than in orbit, and ships beyond the true horizon acos(R/d) are culled.
- **2026-08-04 12:39** · `globe-default` — The washed-out sunny side, measured rather than judged. Sampling the disc and bucketing by how sunlit each point is gave: sun-angle above 0.8 mean 143 sd 27.9, everywhere else mean 93 sd 36-38 — the sub-solar region was fifty per cent brighter with a QUARTER LESS CONTRAST, which is the exponential shoulder doing its job in the wrong place. At an exposure of 1.34 the lit hemisphere sat high on the curve where the slope is lowest, so a threefold difference in incoming light arrived as a 1.5-fold difference on screen and Africa stopped being distinguishable from the sea. Exposure down to 0.98 so the lit side sits in the steep part, plus an S-curve about mid-grey to put back the separation the shoulder costs. Re-measured: sub-solar mean 131 sd 31.0, and contrast is up everywhere else too (sd 39.5 to 43.5). Dimmer AND more contrast, which is what was asked for; the dark side barely moves because it was never the problem.

- **2026-08-04 13:18** · `descent` — The near-field pass no longer wipes the globe. renderer.autoClear is TRUE by default, so the second render() cleared the COLOUR buffer as well as the depth, and every frame of the descent and the Passage threw the backdrop away and drew only the near field over black — what I had been calling the horizon in all of these views was the near-field sky meeting the near-field water. The frame is now composited in the order the world layers: sky, then the Earth where it projects, then the water at your feet, with only DEPTH cleared between passes. The near-field water additionally samples the globe's own elevation field and discards over land, so the coastline in the near field is the same number as the coastline on the map. 3.96 per cent of pixels move because the backdrop is now present at all.
- **2026-08-04 13:54** · `descent` — The near-field GROUND now exists. The globe sphere is 192x128, so a facet is 209 km and at a 195 m eye height the camera sits inside a quarter of one triangle — the planet renders as a flat plate with a straight polygon edge for a horizon, and no shading can put a coast into geometry that is not there. The ground now gets the same treatment the ocean already had: a radial disc anchored under the camera, in the metre-scale scene, DISPLACED by the real elevation field, discarding over water exactly where the sea patch discards over land, so the two tile from the same number. Verified across the Strait of Gibraltar at 260 m: the Moroccan coast rises with a ragged shoreline and a skyline against the sky. The descent frame moves because the near ground and the corrected composite are both present in it.
- **2026-08-04 14:37** · `descent` — The near ground is now lit the way the backdrop is lit. The first version shaded from the real sun and wrote the result out LINEAR, with no tone curve, so a hillside at 0.2 reached the framebuffer as 0.2 — fifty-one of two hundred and fifty-five. The identical value on the globe becomes a hundred and fifteen, because the globe rolls off, gammas and then applies an S-curve. The near ground rendered as a black silhouette against a lit sea: not so much a lighting bug as two different pictures in one frame. It now takes its RELIEF from the same fixed key the globe's land uses, so a ridge reads at any hour, and its BRIGHTNESS from the same day/night term and the same three-line ending — so the near ground and the country behind it are the same country. Verified off the Canaries with land at 852 m: a lit landscape with a real skyline where there had been a silhouette.
- **2026-08-04 14:37** · `descent-coast` — The near ground is now lit the way the backdrop is lit. The first version shaded from the real sun and wrote the result out LINEAR, with no tone curve, so a hillside at 0.2 reached the framebuffer as 0.2 — fifty-one of two hundred and fifty-five. Verified off the Canaries with land at 852 m: a lit landscape with a real skyline where there had been a silhouette.
- **2026-08-04 18:29** · `descent` — Near-field water gains a ground-referenced wave field (uDrift), so it streams past a moving hull instead of travelling with her; the waterline time compression is derived rather than chosen; and the era-5 voyage list is now eight entries. Re-pointed to the Bristol Channel after the pacing change moved every hull's phase.
- **2026-08-04 18:39** · `globe-default` — 62 voyages instead of 13, so the era's fleet is visibly denser; and port labels no longer stack into a vertical column down the limb, where the surface is edge-on and a thousand kilometres of coast projects into a few pixels.
- **2026-08-04 18:39** · `globe-crossing` — Era 0 routes against its OWN shoreline now: the sea stands 68 m lower at 60,000 BP, Sunda is a peninsula, and the tracks follow the ice-age coast rather than the modern one. Plus three more crossings and the limb-label fix.
- **2026-08-04 18:39** · `globe-steam` — Eight era-5 voyages instead of two — the tea race, the Great Eastern's cable, the guano run, the emigrant run, P&O through Suez, the New Bedford whaler — and the limb-label fix.
- **2026-08-04 18:39** · `globe-modern` — Ten Box-era services instead of one, on the lanes that carry the trade: transpacific, Suez, Panama, the 2024 Cape diversion, the Pilbara ore run, the Northern Sea Route. Pacing is proportional to speed now, so every hull sits at a different point of its route at the frozen instant.
- **2026-08-04 18:39** · `ship-container` — TWO changes. This baseline had been photographing the SHIP OF THE LINE — #s=container lost a race against the Shipwright's own default selection, silently — so this is the first time it has shown the ship it is named for. And the stow is no longer a level slab: it steps down forward, the wings are cut back for the sightline from the bridge, and there are hatch covers, lashing bridges, a bulbous bow and a forecastle.
- **2026-08-04 18:39** · `descent-high` — Near-field water gains a ground-referenced wave field, so it streams past a moving hull instead of travelling with her; and the era-5 voyage list is now eight entries.

- **2026-08-04 21:36** · `descent` — Frozen now means frozen for the heading damping, the consort easing and the traffic alteration — all three advanced once per FRAME regardless of dt, so the drawn result depended on how many frames the harness ran before capturing. This frame came back 1.7% different on every run. Two consecutive captures are now byte-identical.
- **2026-08-04 21:36** · `descent-coast` — Same per-frame easing fix.
- **2026-08-05 05:35** · `aboard`, `aboard-off` — NEW. The close-up is the view this project is most often asked about and it had never had a baseline, for the plain reason that no URL could ask for it: `#f=<voyage id>` now goes down to a named ship and follows her, the same way `#c=`/`#z=` made the descent watchable. Two frames rather than one because the view has to hold for a 98 m paddle steamer and a 400 m motor ship and a single frame cannot see that. Both watch the true-scale hull, the near-field water it floats in, her wake, and the join between that water and the sky.
- **2026-08-04 22:09** · `aboard` — Baselined through the tool rather than by hand: my manual copy took an image captured before the frozen-easing fix landed, so the frame reported a 0.47% move against itself. Two consecutive captures are byte-identical.
- **2026-08-04 22:28** · `aboard` — Readiness now waits for the hull to be in the water, not merely for followShip to have been called — the build is deferred a frame on purpose, so a capture could land on an empty near field. That is why this frame differed by 0.47 per cent depending on whether it was captured alone or after eighteen others.

- **2026-08-04 22:52** · `aboard` — See the flap note below.

> ⚠ **OPEN, 2026-08-05: the four near-field frames flap.** `descent`, `descent-high`,
> `descent-coast` and `aboard` each come back about 0.5% of pixels different (mean |Δ| 0.10–0.23,
> so under the mean threshold but over changed_frac) on *some* full runs — and a different one of
> the four each time. Captured on their own, two consecutive runs are byte-identical, so it is
> not the frames and not the GPU: it is something in the near-field path that has two settled
> states depending on which frame of the run it first renders on. Three per-frame easings were
> found and pinned this round (heading damping, consort station, traffic alteration — all
> advanced once per FRAME regardless of dt, which is a clock), and the readiness gate now waits
> for the hull to actually be in the water rather than for followShip to have been called. Both
> reduced it; neither removed it. **Do not accept these frames without looking** — the residue is
> small enough to hide a real change of the same size.
- **2026-08-04 23:47** · `ship-titanic` — SHE HAS A SUPERSTRUCTURE NOW, AND HAD NEVER HAD ONE ON SCREEN. The deckhouse tiers are built about their own centre and their position was never set, so every wall sat at y=0 — below her waterline — while only the railings stood correctly off the deck. What reached the screen was a black hull under a lattice of white rails. Also: four funnels evenly spaced amidships instead of cycling over two mast-gaps and piling up aft, 19.4 m of stack instead of 43.7 (beam x 1.55 was one constant for every ship from a Victorian packet to a battleship), pole masts at their stated heights, and railings on the boat deck only, following the lofted edge instead of a constant half-breadth.
- **2026-08-04 23:47** · `ship-great-eastern` — Funnel height from the record (30 m) rather than beam x 1.55, and a two-deck superstructure that is now above her deck rather than inside her hull.
- **2026-08-04 23:47** · `descent` — Per-frame easing residue; see the flap note in this log.
- **2026-08-04 23:47** · `aboard` — The close-up's far stand-off is 2.6 km instead of 45 km — at 45 km a 57 m hull was a single dark pixel in a featureless field.
- **2026-08-04 23:47** · `aboard-off` — Same.
- **2026-08-04 23:54** · `aboard` — Close-up stand-off reduced to 2.6 km; map floor at 300 km. See the near-field flap note in this log — these frames still settle into one of two states across a full run.

- **2026-08-05 01:04** · `shipwright` — One law for the ripple, across every view that draws water. rippleFade keyed off uScale, which is the EYE HEIGHT — so from a deck the close-up's fine ripple vanished 200 m from the hull while the Shipwright, which hard-codes uScale at 150, kept it to 750. Same shader, same wave table, two different oceans, and the Sea's was the worse one. Whether a ripple is worth drawing is a question about PIXELS: it stops being visible where its wavelength falls below one, which is lambda x screenHeight / (2 tan(fov/2)) away. Computed once in sea.js and handed to the Sea, the Passage, the Shipwright and the Action, so they cannot diverge again.

Whether a ripple is worth drawing is a question about PIXELS: it stops being visible where its wavelength falls below one, which is lambda x screenHeight / (2 tan(fov/2)) away. Computed once in sea.js and handed to the Sea, the Passage, the Shipwright and the Action, so they cannot diverge again.

- **2026-08-05 01:48** · `ship-great-eastern` — Deckhouse walls are DoubleSide, so a hand-lofted shell cannot show holes wherever a face happens to be wound the wrong way — measured, 46 of 72 bearings hit a wall and 26 saw straight through to the funnel bases. And the window band, which was near-black glass over a third of each deck's height and two-thirds of its length, is now a narrower strake of lighter glass with more mullion than pane: four decks of dark stripe alternating with white read as air between unsupported plates. Verified by raycast: 108 of 108 bearings at three heights through the lowest tier, on every ship that has a house.
- **2026-08-05 01:48** · `ship-titanic` — Deckhouse walls are DoubleSide, so a hand-lofted shell cannot show holes wherever a face happens to be wound the wrong way — measured, 46 of 72 bearings hit a wall and 26 saw straight through to the funnel bases. - **2026-08-05 01:48** · `descent` — The near ground has sub-texel relief at last — ridged, domain-warped, added to the ELEVATION rather than to the shading, so the hillshade differentiates the height it is actually drawing. Round 12 tried three times and shipped none of it; the reason those failed is that they perturbed the normal, which belongs to no height field. Amplitude comes from the raster's own vertical variation, computed once at the vertex and carried in, because an amplitude that varied across the hillshade stencil would be read as a slope belonging to nothing. Plus a stated 1.8x vertical exaggeration, so a low coast clears the horizon at all.

- **2026-08-05 23:57** · `descent-high` — Whitecaps are intermittent along a crest, not continuous. Foam was laid on every crest of a four-component Gerstner sum, and four sinusoids are visibly periodic once resolved — so the white drew the periodicity and the ocean became a lattice of pale dashes under magnification. The lace meant to break it ran at one cycle per 16.7 m, far below a pixel at these zooms, so it averaged to a constant and let foam through everywhere. Measured by FFT: peak-to-mean 395 to 139.
- **2026-08-05 23:57** · `map-floor` — Whitecaps are intermittent along a crest, not continuous. Foam was laid on every crest of a four-component Gerstner sum, and four sinusoids are visibly periodic once resolved — so the white drew the periodicity and the ocean became a lattice of pale dashes under magnification. The lace meant to break it ran at one cycle per 16.7 m, far below a pixel at these zooms, so it averaged to a constant and let foam through everywhere. Measured by FFT: peak-to-mean 395 to 139.

- **2026-08-06 00:19** · `ship-great-eastern` — The container ship's funnel now rises from an engine casing that reaches the deck instead of hanging 17.2 m from the nearest part of the ship with its base 47 m up; and the boat-deck rails follow the same lofted deck edge as their own stanchions, from which they had come adrift when the posts were moved onto the taper and the rails were left at a constant half-breadth. - **2026-08-06 03:45** · `ship-carrier` — The island was a box with a cylinder on top, which is why the survey ranked this ship crudest in the fleet — 337 m in fifty-four meshes. The flight deck was not the reason: its markings, arrestor wires, catapult tracks and flush lifts were already there. An island is the only part of a carrier allowed to be tall, so its shape is a list of what cannot go under an armoured flat deck: navigating bridge low and forward, flying control above and aft where a controller can see the landing area, uptakes carried up through the middle, and a mast whose job is height for the radar. Three stepped tiers each standing on the one below, glass where the bridge and pri-fly actually are, two uptakes, four fixed radar faces and a mast with yardarms.

This frame's bar changed step accordingly.

- **2026-08-07 01:51** · `globe-default` — Round 41 (interactive) plus round 40's paddle box, landed together. Causes, all deliberate: (1) floatShip rebuilt — heave is now the exact mean of the surface over the waterplane and pitch/roll its first moments, sinc(k.mu.L/2), so every hull sits and moves differently and the big ones barely at all: carrier heave range 0.21 m against the dugout's 4.35 m in the same sea. (2) Era titles and ledes rewritten toward description, which changes the strip in every globe and sea frame ('The box' -> 'Containers'). (3) The left column is measured now, not assumed — readout, then ship's slip, then card — so #psgCard no longer buries #readout and #card no longer runs under the era strip. (4) Shipwright: nine hulls held at full detail around the selection instead of one, so neighbours have rudders; the zoom carries between ships; the fleet strip scrolls visibly and centres the selection. (5) ship-great-eastern additionally carries round 40's paddle box, rebuilt as a closed drum after its faces were found wound inward and culled from every bearing. Audit 25/25 including the new 30-ray paddlebox test.

- **2026-08-07 19:39** · `globe-default` — Three causes, all deliberate. (1) THE SEA VIEW HAD NO LIGHTS AT ALL — zero light objects in the scene — so every MeshStandardMaterial hull rendered black and the fleet was flat cut-outs of correctly-detailed models; the globe, ocean and atmosphere are ShaderMaterials carrying their own sun uniform, which is why nothing ever revealed it. A key and a hemisphere now, both driven from the same sunVector(S.month) the water is drawn with. (2) The square-sail belly is mirrored at the vertex after the cloth detail, so canvas sets FORWARD of the yard instead of abaft it — verified on preussen from a low bearing: full courses, canvas ahead of the masts. (3) Consorts wander around their station instead of holding a rigid formation, per ship, phased so no two agree. Plus the era cards rewritten with record rows, descriptive Shipwright headings, and the card no longer overlapping the readout.

- **2026-08-07 21:03** · `aboard` — The wake rebuilt. The straight lines August photographed were three step() calls — step(0.0, along) began the turbulent band with a razor edge across her heading, step(fwd, uWakeLen*0.60) chopped the bow wave off ahead of the stem, step(uWakeLen*0.25, along) did the same for the arms. Every edge is a smoothstep over her own beam now. The band starts at the STERN rather than at the ship's centre; the bow wave is a crescent measured from the stem; transverse crests were missing entirely and are added at lambda = 2*pi*V^2/g, which is why a slow boat leaves close-set ripples and a fast ship long swells. And foam is separated from wave-making: Froude scaling is right for the Kelvin arms and wrong for white water, which a hull breaks at any speed she is moving — the same factor was also being applied twice, which is how a 4.3 kn ship ended up with 7% of a wake.

And foam is separated from wave-making: Froude scaling is right for the Kelvin arms and wrong for white water, which a hull breaks at any speed she is moving — the same factor was also being applied twice, which is how a 4.3 kn ship ended up with 7% of a wake.
- **2026-08-07 21:03** · `aboard-clipper` — The wake rebuilt. The straight lines August photographed were three step() calls — step(0.0, along) began the turbulent band with a razor edge across her heading, step(fwd, uWakeLen*0.60) chopped the bow wave off ahead of the stem, step(uWakeLen*0.25, along) did the same for the arms. Every edge is a smoothstep over her own beam now. The band starts at the STERN rather than at the ship's centre; the bow wave is a crescent measured from the stem; transverse crests were missing entirely and are added at lambda = 2*pi*V^2/g, which is why a slow boat leaves close-set ripples and a fast ship long swells. And foam is separated from wave-making: Froude scaling is right for the Kelvin arms and wrong for white water, which a hull breaks at any speed she is moving — the same factor was also being applied twice, which is how a 4.3 kn ship ended up with 7% of a wake.

- **2026-08-09 19:51** · `globe-default` — Daylight theme plus the eight items and the splash texts removed: palette inverted to chart paper with globe lettering deliberately NOT inverted (it sits on dark water); background needed setClearColor AND scene.background because this view renders two passes with autoClear=false; containers on the coarse hull so the map stops drawing box boats as bare hulls; the voyage card unblocked in the close-up (body.in-passage #card was display:none); consorts sail with her there; Shipwright part cards removed, fleet strip chronological, zoom-out 8x to 26x; and landward() reaching 900 km instead of 140 with the coast drawn compressed into the near field and named from the port gazetteer.

- **2026-08-09 20:32** · `aboard` — Contrast fixes for the daylight theme in the close-up, plus the consorts. .c-prose was cream (#d6cfbc) — a dark-panel reading colour that washes out on paper; #psgCard predates the shared .panel class and carried its own night palette so the close-up kept the old theme in one box; body.in-passage #voyList dimmed to .28, which was tuned against a dark ground and disappears on a light one. And the consorts finally appear: the first attempt put them in psgOpen(), which is defined and never called.

- **2026-08-12 01:04** · `ship-queen-mary-2` — wlPower corrected on Queen Mary 2 and Azzam. That parameter governs the parallel middle body, not the entry: low p tapers continuously from amidships and makes a spindle of a long hull, high p holds full beam and fines only at the ends. Both were set low on L/B ~8.5 hulls, which is the thinness. 3.8 and 3.5 now, with the fineness moved to stemFineness where it acts. Azzam's cm also 0.68 (deep V) to 0.86, since she draws 4.3 m on 20.8 m of beam.
- **2026-08-12 01:04** · `ship-azzam` — wlPower corrected on Queen Mary 2 and Azzam. That parameter governs the parallel middle body, not the entry: low p tapers continuously from amidships and makes a spindle of a long hull, high p holds full beam and fines only at the ends. Both were set low on L/B ~8.5 hulls, which is the thinness. 3.8 and 3.5 now, with the fineness moved to stemFineness where it acts. Azzam's cm also 0.68 (deep V) to 0.86, since she draws 4.3 m on 20.8 m of beam.

- **2026-08-12 02:07** · `ship-panokseon` — NEW: the 32nd hull, round 89 - the panokseon (board-roofed ship, 1555, Myeongnyang 1597). The gunDeck class learned its second law: no apostis exists, so the fighting deck stands on stanchions on the hull's own rail, lofted along the rail line with overhanging beam-ends; chest-high plank bulwark with gunports (muzzles pierce, dark port frames on the outer face), closed athwartships at both ends; janggundae tower (posts, railed platform, hipped roof) at 0.62; 9 sculling oars a side pivoting BELOW the deck; two battened-lug masts; junk-family median rudder off the bulkhead build. - **2026-08-12 03:40** · `ship-sekibune` — NEW: the 33rd hull, the sekibune - so-yagura on the r89 gunDeck class with sama loopholes (record-driven GD.loops, audited by count and band), yakata tower, one-tier square mast without shrouds, oars awash; looked at from 12 bearings before commit
- **2026-08-13 02:11** · `ship-great-eastern` — Deck height is headroom, not a fraction of beam. The default dh = B*0.105 is right for Titanic's 28.2 m beam and coincidence elsewhere: Queen Mary 2's 41 m gave 4.30 m decks, 45% too tall, so ten decks made 43 m of house on 17 m of freeboard. Default is now min(B*0.105, 3.0) with every existing decked hull pinned in the data to its current value, so only the two that were wrong move.

- **2026-08-16 18:18** · `ship-queen-mary-2` — Round 93: the hull is REBUILT from a measured plate, plus round 92's sea behind her. Queen Mary 2's house plan, flush sides (houseTaper), 45 m bridge wings (bridgeBeamM), 24 m oval funnel at u 0.570 and boats stowed inboard all come from segmenting the Commons scale profile at every metre of height; Azzam's mast, four-deck house and u-spans from a clean Bremen broadside at 8.33 px/m. Verified part by part in metres with Research/measure_ship.py against the plate readings, not by eye.
- **2026-08-16 18:18** · `ship-azzam` — Round 93: the hull is REBUILT from a measured plate, plus round 92's sea behind her. Queen Mary 2's house plan, flush sides (houseTaper), 45 m bridge wings (bridgeBeamM), 24 m oval funnel at u 0.570 and boats stowed inboard all come from segmenting the Commons scale profile at every metre of height; Azzam's mast, four-deck house and u-spans from a clean Bremen broadside at 8.33 px/m. Verified part by part in metres with Research/measure_ship.py against the plate readings, not by eye.
- **2026-08-16 18:19** · `shipwright` — Round 93: round 92's Shipwright sea, finally ratcheted. That round put the view on a real ocean (radial disc to the horizon, hazed shore, sky), removed the 'take a ship apart' subtitle and reordered the fleet bar, and its own capture reported 'captured 26, moved 0' — an anomaly flagged at the time and never chased, so 27 baselines carried the OLD flat tile until now. The diff is proof the hull did not move: Titanic and Dreadnought read pure black over the whole hull and rig, with every moved pixel in the sea, the horizon, the ghosted subtitle and the fleet list. No hull without houseTaper/funnelOval/bridgeBeamM/boatsRecessed in its record can move: at taper 0.16 the new width formula is 0.92 - i/n*0.16 exactly, and every other new branch is guarded on a key only Queen Mary 2 and Azzam carry.

The diff is proof the hull did not move: Titanic and Dreadnought read pure black over the whole hull and rig, with every moved pixel in the sea, the horizon, the ghosted subtitle and the fleet list. No hull without houseTaper/funnelOval/bridgeBeamM/boatsRecessed in its record can move: at taper 0.16 the new width formula is 0.92 - i/n*0.16 exactly, and every other new branch is guarded on a key only Queen Mary 2 and Azzam carry.

- **2026-08-16 21:25** · `ship-queen-mary-2` — Round 94: the three faults August named, each fixed at its cause. FLICKER — the hull's capping rail ran the whole length on every hull, so on a ship whose house carries out to the shell it sat 16 cm outboard of the white wall: z-fighting by construction, a torn crawling line along the strake. Proved by hiding fittings one at a time under a fixed camera; the rail is the only one whose removal takes it away. A rail caps a deck EDGE and there is none where the superstructure IS the ship's side, so it is emitted only over open spans. SHAPE — her balcony pier is 12% of a 2.6 m pitch and was drawn by painting one station white, but a colour on a vertex has no edge: it bled a full quad each way and arrived three times too wide, so she read as a lattice of pale boxes. snapBand inserts a station PAIR a millimetre apart at every pier boundary; measured on the render, 87% dark / 10% light against the recorded 88/12. DISPROPORTION — 220 m of blank top deck carrying one funnel and a wire; topWorks restores the seven structures the silhouette segmentation had already measured and thrown away, and the mast steps on the crest instead of running up through ten decks of accommodation. The audit caught the masthead at 63.8 m against Cunard's attested 62 and was right: the record wins over a derivation from it.
- **2026-08-16 21:25** · `ship-azzam` — Round 94: a steel deck instead of a teak beach (the deckIsSteel heuristic gives a timber deck to every steel hull without containers or a flight deck, which is right for Titanic and Yamato and wrong for a 2013 motor yacht); a stem that reaches — she measured 174.8 m against her own recorded 180.6; and railed promenades on her forward tier roofs. That last is the general fix: the rail rule railed only the AFT exposed roof, because the liner it was written for cascades aft and crests forward. A motor yacht is built the other way about, so her three forward roofs came out as bare white plates 14 m long — a stack of paving stones, and the loudest thing wrong with her from above. A roof you can stand on carries a rail whichever end of the ship it faces.
- **2026-08-16 21:25** · `shipwright` — Round 94: the Shipwright's shore, and the tab order. LAND MUST END WHERE IT GOES UNDER, NOT WHERE THE MESH RUNS OUT — the shore was a 63-degree arc at a constant 120 m minimum, so both ends were vertical walls standing 200 m out of the sea in mid-air, which is the grey slab that simply stopped at the end of the ship line. Widening the arc only moves the wall. It is a CLOSED RING now (no ends exist to be cut) whose height is a landness envelope spending most of its circumference below zero; clamped at zero those stretches are open sea and the crossings are headlands receding under the curve. Phases searched against the sector this camera actually sees, so the coast rises out of the water inside the frame. Land over 48% of the ring. Also: the Shipwright opens by default, so it is first in the tab line — a list still reading Sea/Shipwright/Action put the lit tab in the middle. And the capping-rail cut reaches every hull whose shell tier stands flush: on Titanic that removes a sub-pixel shimmer along B deck, where the plating genuinely carried up flush and there was no railed deck at the sheer. Diffs confirm the class: on Titanic and the dugout every moved pixel is in the top third (4.0% and 2.9%) with the bottom third at 0.00% — the horizon and the tabs, not the hulls.
